# Structure note: the cottage cheese redo (replaces "9 Cottage Cheese Brands Canadians MUST AVOID IMMEDIATELY!", Feb 25 2026)

Prepared 30 Sep 2026. Word and character counts come from a script (scratchpad/measure.py) run on cleaned transcripts, with `[music]`, `[snorts]` and `>>` stripped. They are measured, not estimated. Beat boundaries are verbatim anchor phrases, listed in measure.py. Every fact in sections 2, 5 and 6 carries the URL that was opened and the date it was opened (30 Sep 2026 unless stated). Anything that could not be opened at tier (a)/(b)/(d) is in section 7, UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE.

House rules applied throughout (hard):
- No health, nutrition, protein, calorie, fat-as-health, sodium, probiotic, additive-safety, gut or food-safety content.
- Recalls appear only in Appendix A and are not for use on air.
- Label and regulation text is quoted literally. No ingredient is characterised as good or bad.
- Tiers: (a) primary, (b) named outlet with byline and date, (c) aggregators, Wikipedia and review sites (flagged, never a source), (d) named survey with methodology.
- Prices come only from a retailer's own site or API. Each carries the banner, store or region, date, pack size, price and a computed $/100 g.
- Nothing states or implies wrongdoing or motive.
- No U.S. data is presented as Canadian.
- Taste and texture are opinion.

## 1. Measured skeletons

### 1a. Cheese (99K, 15.3x median). The construction to copy. 3,613 words, 21,553 chars

| Beat | Words | Share | Starts at (% words) | Chars | Starts at (% chars) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| Close: Ingersoll mammoth cheese, falling processed share, price gap | 179 | 5.0% | 92.1% | 1,050 | 92.4% |
| Comment ask + share ask + sign-off | 105 | 2.9% | 97.1% | 592 | 97.3% |

Cold-open moves (400 w), in order:
1. A single word as the hook, in the first sentence: "There is a single word printed on some of the most popular cheese in Canada. And once you see it, you cannot unsee it. The word is product."
2. The legal reading of that word: "it is not a style choice. It is a legal confession."
3. The standard, quoted and numbered: "at least 83% of the protein must come from actual fluid milk".
4. The ladder, rung by rung (processed cheddar; cheese food "legally only 51% cheese"; spread "up to 60% moisture"; "processed cheese product. A name that appears nowhere in the cheese standards at all").
5. Scale and money: "Over 15 kg per person a year", "$5.80 in 2017 now averages about $7", "passing $7 billion a year".
6. The method claim: "Every claim from the company's own Canadian product pages... the Canadian Food Inspection Agency standards, Statistics Canada, and actual court records."
7. Teases with facts: "this aisle has been to court and even across the border in the trunk of a police officer's car".
8. The count and the payoff promise: "12 famous names... Then, eight that are gloriously, verifiably the real thing... By the end, you will sort any cheese shelf in this country in 5 seconds flat."

There is no ask in the open and no health language anywhere in the open.

Per-beat anatomy, #1 Kraft Singles (322 w):
- Recognition (15): "the orange square that built a million grilled cheese sandwiches".
- The name on the maker's own page (45): "Kraft's own Canadian website titles the Swiss variety, in Kraft's own words, 'processed cheese product slices.'"
- The ingredient line read in order (60): "Modified milk ingredients first, water second, cheese... third".
- Definition of a label term (55): "Modified milk ingredients... is the catch-all Canadian label term..."
- The regulator's current action (25): "The CFIA is actually consulting on renaming the class".
- Concession (65): "Nothing on the Kraft label is hidden... to be fair to the engineers, the recipe does what it is designed to do."
- Price per kg against the real block (55): "roughly $14 a kilogram, while a block of real Black Diamond old cheddar runs about 19".
- Closer (10): "You are not saving what you think you are saving."

There is no owner line; ownership is saved for #4.

#4 Black Diamond slices (227 w):
- Recognition and heritage from the brand's own account (35): "founded in 1933. By its own account, the first brand named cheddar".
- The designation read slowly (40): "cheddar style processed cheese slices. Style."
- Ingredient line (25).
- The twist: the same logo sells the real thing (45): "One logo, two completely different foods, separated by one word on the front and about a dollar."
- Ownership with a year and price (45): "all left Kraft in 2019, sold for $1.6 billion to Lactalis".
- Closer (15): "The most Canadian names in the cheese aisle answer to Paris now."

The store-brand block (163 w): three house brands as one beat, each reduced to its designation and first three ingredients. The concession is stated: "None of these are scandals. All of them are printed exactly as the law demands."

The "real" turn at 68.4%: "Now, the eight that are exactly what they claim". The first real pick is a reversal the channel admits: "The lunchbox snack this channel fully expected to put on the avoid list turns out to be real cheese... We read the label expecting a villain and found real cheese. So, real cheese is what we're reporting." Each real pick is built as:
- designation or first ingredients;
- owner, and whether it is a co-op, with year and place;
- one number (price, members, km radius);
- closer.

The close (284 w from 92.1%):
- A dated national story (the 1866 Ingersoll "7,300 lb wheel").
- The statistical trend: "Processed cheese's share of Canadian retail is falling."
- The price gap: "roughly the price of a coffee per kilogram".
- The comment ask as a data request: "tell us in the comments exactly what your package says: cheese, cheese food, or cheese product... the national fridge audit".
- The share ask: "If your family has bought the same orange slices for 30 years, send them this video".
- Sign-off line: "read the designation before you trust the diamond."

There is no disclaimer and no tagline.

Asks: subscribe at 42.1% chars, like at 48.8% chars, comment and share at 97.3% chars. The subscribe ask carries the credibility line "Canadian Counter reads Canadian labels, standards, and fine print every single week. No scare stories."

| Beat | Words | Share | Starts at (% words) | Chars | Starts at (% chars) |

Cold-open moves:
- It opens on the standard, not on a hook word: "Under Canada's federal food standards, butter is one thing and one thing only... at least 80% milk fat".
- Then prices ("250 g blocks selling for $9").
- Then three fact-teases: a trunk-smuggled butter, a lawsuit, "the winter our butter stopped softening".
- Method line.
- Count and order: "Starting with the most popular butter in the country".
- An explicit no-fear promise: "Nothing tonight is dangerous... This is not about fear."

Legal items are labelled exactly: "Those are allegations, not findings. Nothing has been proven in court"; Fonterra "settled... accepted the packaging was likely to mislead"; Kerrygold "a federal judge dismissed the case".

The four-step shelf check at 71.2% matches the house four-step form, and step four is "check the price per 100 g on the shelf tag".

## 2. What the cheese video's "real vs not real" test was, and whether it transfers

### 2a. The test, in the video's own words

It is a two-part designation test, with milk origin used only as a tie-breaker among the real picks.

1. The designation, the legal name under the brand. The video's words: "Under the brand name, find the designation, the small words that legally define the food. Cheese or a variety name like cheddar or mozzarella. Real, processed cheese, melted real cheese, cheese food, cheese spread, majority rule, 51% cheese product, below every standard in the book." Also: "The word product is the industry quietly telling you, this does not qualify." And: "The designation is law."
2. The first ingredient: "Then flip it and read the first ingredient. Real cheese starts with milk. The imitations start with modified milk ingredients and water."
3. The standard's numbers as proof: "For cheddar, at least 83% of the protein must come from actual fluid milk". S2 7.4.3(1)(a) actually says casein "derived from milk or from ultrafiltered milk, partly skimmed milk, ultrafiltered partly skimmed milk, skim milk, ultrafiltered skim milk or cream, rather than from other milk products, that is at least 83% of the total protein content". The video's "fluid milk" wording is a paraphrase. The pizza mozzarella numbers (58% moisture, 15% fat, 63%) match S2 7.4.2 exactly.
4. Milk origin: supporting only. "blue cow logo, 100% Canadian milk" (Cheese Strings); Kirkland "carry the blue cow"; "The Canadian Cheese Awards... will not even accept an entry containing modified milk ingredients." It is not the pass/fail test.
5. Ownership: colour, not the test ("The most Canadian names in the cheese aisle answer to Paris now").

It is not a nutrition test, and it does not use the word "healthy" anywhere. That is why it is the right model for this channel's rules.

### 2b. Does it transfer to cottage cheese? Partly, and the partial answer is the script's best material

| Cheese-video test element | Cottage cheese equivalent (S2, S1, retailer pages, 30 Sep 2026) | Transfers? |
|---|---|---|
| A ladder of legal names (cheese, processed cheese, cheese food, spread, "product") | The standard has only two names. 7.4.5: "Cottage Cheese is the product, in the form of discrete curd particles, prepared from skim milk, evaporated skim milk or skim milk powder and harmless acid-producing bacterial cultures; (a) contains not more than 80% moisture; and (b) may contain (i) milk, (ii) cream, (iii) milk powder, (iv) salt, (v) added lactose, (vi) relishes, (vii) fruits, and (viii) vegetables." 7.4.6: "Creamed Cottage Cheese is cottage cheese containing cream or a mixture of cream with milk or skim milk, or both, in such quantity that the final product and contains (a) not less than 4% milk fat; and (b) not more than 80% moisture." | Partly. There is no ladder, but there is an outside-the-standard name |
| "Processed cheese product... appears nowhere in the cheese standards" | "Cottage cheese product" appears nowhere in the FCSD or the FDR (full-text searches of S1 and S2, 30 Sep 2026). Yet Sealtest's own site names all three tubs "Cottage Cheese Product" (S14). The Sealtest 2% tub front on Voilà reads "LOW FAT COTTAGE CHEESE PRODUCT" (S30 image). Nordica Smooth's pack reads "1.7% M.F. COTTAGE CHEESE PRODUCT" (S19) | Yes. The same device, with a Canadian dairy co-op's tub |
| The 83% / 95% casein-from-milk rule | Does not apply. 7.4.2's exclusion list ends "...cold-pack cheese food with (naming the added ingredients), cottage cheese and creamed cottage cheese". Cottage cheese is outside the casein rule | No. Do not say "83%" in this script |
| "Read the first ingredient: milk vs modified milk ingredients" | Most tubs open with the class name "Milk ingredients". Under S5 Table 2 item 7 that class is "butter, buttermilk, butter oil, milk fat, cream, milk, partly skimmed milk and skim milk". Some makers list each milk part separately instead (Sealtest, Island Farms, Liberté, dry curd tubs). Nordica Fat-Free and Gay Lea Fat-Free open with "Modified milk ingredients". That class is being renamed "milk-derived ingredients" (S5, last amended Feb 11, 2026; old name allowed "until January 1, 2030") | Weakly. The first-ingredient test only separates the fat-free tubs. The whole-line test works better: does the line contain only what 7.4.5 names (skim milk, culture; may contain milk, cream, milk powder, salt, lactose), or does it also list items authorised through Health Canada's separate additive lists (S4)? State it as a comparison of lists, never as good or bad |
| M.F. and moisture on the front | Cottage cheese and creamed cottage cheese must show "the percentage of milk fat... followed by the words 'milk fat' or the abbreviation 'B.F.' or 'M.F.'" (S1 B.08.002.3(k),(l)). They are exempt from the moisture statement other cheeses carry (S1 B.08.032(1)). The standard's moisture cap is 80% (S2) | Yes. "The number on the front is law" |
| Milk origin / blue cow | Blue cow listings exist for Sealtest, Island Farms, Nordica, Northumberland (S32). Dairyland and Neilson state "using milk from Canadian farms" (S10, S11; company says). The over-access tariff on fresh cheese and curd is "245.5% but not less than $4.52/kg" (S9). Origin barely separates brands | Only as a step-three check, not as the verdict |
| Ownership as colour | Four owners hold nearly every tub: Loblaw, Sobeys/Empire, Saputo, Agropur and Gay Lea (sections 5b, 6c). Saputo sells the same ingredient items under three regional names (S10, S11, S30) | Yes. Use it as the order basis |

Transfer verdict: build the video on the designation device ("cottage cheese" vs "creamed cottage cheese" vs "cottage cheese product") plus the whole ingredient line read against the 7.4.5 list. Do not use the casein rule. Do not treat the first ingredient as the verdict. Treat milk origin as a check, not the thesis.

One hard caution. S32 lists Sealtest cottage cheese as "Cottage Cheese" and Island Farms as "Cottage Cheese Product". That is the reverse of both brands' own sites on 30 Sep 2026. Names on tubs change. The writer must photograph each tub named on air, front and ingredient panel, on the shoot date, and read the name from that photograph.

## 3. Diagnosis: cream flop (2.6K) versus cheese hit (100K). Factual, from titles, thumbnails and transcripts only

| Factor | Cheese (100K) | Cream (2.6K) |
|---|---|---|
| Title noun | 'These 12 "Cheese" Brands'. The scare-quoted noun is the thing the video tests, and the thing the viewer buys | 'These 7 "Cream" Brands'. The video's avoid list is coffee whiteners and creamers (Coffee-mate, International Delight, store "coffee whitener", Silk "coffee enhancer", Earth's Own oat creamer). The only "cream" items are in the winners list |
| Title parenthetical | "(8 That Are ACTUALLY Real Cheese)": a count of real items, larger than usual | "(Real Cream Is ACTUALLY Cheaper)": a price claim, no count of real items |
| Thumbnail text vs title | "THIS IS NOT CHEESE", the same noun as the title | "THIS IS NOT CREAMER!", a different noun from the title ("Cream") |
| Thumbnail product vs verdict | A Black Diamond Old Cheddar block. The video later calls these blocks "real cheddar" (R2), so this is a mismatch too | A Carnation Evaporated Milk can spilling. The video says "the can is real milk... closer to real dairy than almost anyone buying creamer today". The pictured product is one the video clears. The butter hit did the same (Lactantia "THIS IS NOT BUTTER"; the video says Lactantia's list is "cream and salt, exactly as the law requires"). The mismatch alone does not separate hit from flop |
| Opening sentence | A word printed on the product ("The word is product") and the legal ladder | "You've been whitening your coffee your whole life." Then "71% of Canadians drank coffee yesterday", with no source named in the video |
| Distinct avoid beats | 8 brand beats plus 2 history beats. Store brands grouped once | 7 "brands" in 4 beats (#3–5 grouped, #6–7 grouped). The cream standard explainer comes after #1, at 23.4% |
| Where the "real" list starts | 68.4% of words, 8 picks, all real brands | 73.7% of words, 3 picks. Winner #1 is "make your own", not a product |
| Nutrition content | None | A front-of-pack symbol aside (153 w: "5 g of sugars", "saturated fat trigger") and sugar grams in #2 and #6–7 ("5 gram of sugars per 15ml tablespoon") |
| Topic breadth | The whole cheese case: slices, blocks, shreds, curds, feta | One use-case (coffee whitener), one aisle end-cap |
| Neighbouring uploads | Aug 13 butter 85K, Aug 11 ice cream 31K, Aug 15 burgers 37K | Aug 30 noodles 64K, Sep 3 ketchup 90K. Posting slot and cadence were similar |
| Sister flop in the same construction | none | Sep 11 'We Investigated 9 "Cream Cheese" Brands In Canada (Only 4 Are Legally CREAM CHEESE)', 2.8K. A standard-of-identity title alone did not carry that one either |

What the data supports:
- In the flop, the scare-quoted title noun, the thumbnail noun and the tested category do not match each other ("Cream" / "CREAMER" / whiteners).
- In both hits, they match ("Cheese" / "CHEESE" / cheese; "Butter" / "BUTTER" / butter).
- The hits count their real items in the title; the flop does not.
- The hit tests an everyday staple; the flop tests a narrow sub-category.

For cottage cheese:
- Keep the noun identical in title, thumbnail and test ("Cottage Cheese").
- Put a count of real ones in the parenthetical.
- Do not put a product on the thumbnail that the video later clears.

That last point is also required by house rule 6. A thumbnail saying a named product is "not" something is an on-air claim.

---

## 4. Every line in the February video the new script must not repeat

Sentence numbers refer to scratchpad/feb_sentences.txt (189 sentences, cleaned transcript).

### 4a. Health, nutrition, protein, sodium, "clean", calorie, bone and "into your body" lines (rule 1): 38 lines, all barred

- s1: "it is now seen as one of the healthiest dairy options in the grocery store."
- s2: "Packed with protein, low in carbohydrates... the perfect choice for anyone trying to eat clean."
- s5: "Behind those high protein labels, many popular brands are loaded with unnecessary additives, modified starches, artificial stabilizers, excess sodium, and industrial dairy sourcing."
- s10: "That directly affects the nutritional profile of the final product."
- s11: "...based on ingredient quality, sourcing transparency, sodium levels, and overall processing."
- s13: "If you care about what goes into your body..."
- s35: "While it delivers protein at a competitive price point..."
- s45: "many healthconscious consumers actively avoid it."
- s64: "Noame delivers protein at the lowest possible cost."
- s65: "this brand offers little beyond basic nutrition."
- s72–73: "Ultra filtered skim milk is milk that has been pushed through fine membranes to concentrate the protein content. Essentially an industrial process to boost protein numbers on the label."
- s76: "...designed for specific protein and texture targets..."
- s79–80: "These sodium levels can be elevated... For consumers eating cottage cheese daily as a protein staple, this cumulative sodium intake becomes nutritionally relevant."
- s90: "...what makes cottage cheese a wholesome, minimally processed food."
- s107: "Sodium content is comparable to competitors, meaning regular consumption contributes meaningfully to daily salt intake."
- s108: "...provides protein at a reasonable price... rather than consumer health."
- s116: "Maltodextrin has a high glycemic index, meaning it spikes blood sugar quickly and offers no nutritional benefit beyond empty calories."
- s123: "Adequate protein delivery at competitive pricing..."
- s135: "Island Farms Cottage Cheese delivers adequate nutrition..."
- s137: "...many healthconscious Canadians assume is the safer choice."
- s153: "...make informed choices about what they put in their bodies."
- s155: "But if you still want high protein dairy without the unnecessary additives..."
- s175: "Nordica is made from 100% Canadian dairy milk, provides calcium for bone health, and is both gluten and nut-free."
- s177: "For healthconscious Canadians..."
- s179: "Ingredient lists, sodium levels, milk sourcing, and processing methods..."
- s182: "While they deliver protein, they do not always deliver the cleaner formulation..."
- s185: "They impact taste, texture, and overall nutritional value."
- s187: "...not all high protein dairy is created equally."
- The title and description: "not worth your money or your health", "health buzzwords", "excessive sodium".
- The thumbnail word "DEADLY".

### 4b. Additive characterisations: harmful, chemical, cheap, engineered, "unnecessary" (rule 1): 39 lines, all barred

- s6: "gums and thickeners designed to mask poor milk quality"
- s7: "heavily processed production methods that strip away natural texture and flavor"
- s22: "That is a substantial list of additives for something that should be relatively simple."
- s24–26 (gums): "...indicates a product engineered for shelf stability..."
- s27: "Mono and diglycerides are emulsifiers, which are chemicals... similar to how dish soap cuts through grease."
- s28: "...their presence signals heavy processing."
- s30: "enzymes produced by bacteria or fungi in a lab setting"
- s41: "That is an extensive list of stabilizers and emulsifiers."
- s42–44: "Kagenan deserves particular attention... some studies suggest it may cause digestive inflammation in sensitive individuals."
- s46–48: "Polyorbate 80... a synthetic emulsifier..."
- s74–75: "Kyosin B is a lab produced enzyme... indicates a highly controlled industrial production process."
- s76: "these engineered ingredients"
- s77–78: "The multiple acids and emulsifiers suggest a product optimized for long shelf life"
- s103–104: "Multiple gums for texture control, emulsifiers for uniformity, and carbon dioxide injection for extended freshness."
- s106: "None of these ingredients are necessary in traditionally made cottage cheese."
- s113–115: "The presence of maltodextrin is notable. This is a highly processed white powder... used as a cheap filler"
- s117–119: "Natural flavor is another ingredient worth questioning... hundreds of different chemical compounds... leaving consumers unable to evaluate what they are actually consuming."
- s120–121: "Potassium sorbate is a chemical preservative... engineered for industrial distribution"
- s130–131: skim milk powder "suggests standardization... moves the product away from simple... fresh preparation"
- s132–134: "Polyorbate 80 appears once again. This synthetic emulsifier... While regulatory bodies consider it safe..."
- s140–146: "organic does not automatically mean free from processing additives... The gums are organic but they are still gums... texture modifiers and stabilizers..."
- s150: "Free from processing aids entirely."
- s160: "the more concerning additives like keragenan and polyorbate 80"
- s171: "Gums are still present reflecting the realities of modern dairy distribution."

### 4c. Food-safety lines (rule 1): barred

- s105: "Carbon dioxide is pumped into the packaging to slow bacterial growth..."
- s120: potassium sorbate "prevents mold and yeast growth, extending shelf life far beyond what fresh dairy would naturally last".

Where a label reads "Carbon dioxide (to maintain product freshness)", read it verbatim if needed. Do not explain it.

### 4d. Taste and texture asserted as fact (rule 8): barred unless attributed to the company

- s34: "The flavor tends to be salty and somewhat artificial in its creaminess."
- s63: "The texture can vary between batches, sometimes watery, sometimes overly thick..."
- s91–93: "The kurds often appear uniform and engineered... The texture feels thick and stabilized. Flavor tends towards salty..."
- s163: "The texture tends to be creamier and more natural..."
- s7 (in 4b).

Permitted form, attributed: Island Farms "says its cottage cheese has 'larger, more distinct curds'" (S15).

### 4f. The template overlap (do not reuse any February open or close sentence)

A script-measured 8-gram overlap between the February cleaned transcript and the U.S. "Consumed Daily" transcript (tr_CD_cottage_280K.txt):
- 72.8% of 8-grams in February's first 231 words (open + ask) also appear in the U.S. video.
- 74.3% of 8-grams in February's last 178 words (close + ask) do too.
- The titles share "... MUST AVOID IMMEDIATELY! (AVOID At All COSTS!)" / "To AVOID IMMEDIATELY! (AVOID At All COSTS!)".

This is a measurement of text overlap, not a finding about who wrote what first. The new script must share no sentence with either.

### 4g. Verdicts the new script reverses, with the on-air acknowledgement

February ranked Nordica the #1 pick (s165–177). The new skeleton places Nordica Fat-Free and Nordica Smooth on the avoid list (5h #8, #9), judged by their names and ingredient lines. Acknowledge it once, in the Nordica Fat-Free beat, 25 words:

"In February this channel called Nordica the best tub on the shelf. We judged on the wrong things. Tonight we judge by the name and the list."

Organic Meadow and Liberté, February's #9 avoid and #2 pick, drop out: Organic Meadow has no retailer page opened (U12), and Liberté's owner is unverified and its site lists no cottage cheese (U13).

---

## 5. Recommended skeleton for the new script

### 5a. Targets

21,000–23,000 characters of plain spoken prose. The register to copy is the cheese video's, at 5.97 chars/word. At 5.95 chars/word the skeleton below is 3,775 words, about 22,460 characters. If the writer runs at 6.2 chars/word (the February script ran 6.80 on additive vocabulary), trim #1, #6 and R1 by 50 words each to hold 23,000.

Suggested title, mirroring the cheese hit, with nouns matched (section 3):
'Canadians Must AVOID These 9 "Cottage Cheese" Brands (3 That Are ACTUALLY Real Cottage Cheese)'

"Real" here means real under 7.4.5 as defined on air. The writer should confirm the three picks survive the tub photographs before locking the title.

Thumbnail:
- The word the tub prints, not a verdict: 'IT SAYS "PRODUCT"' over a Sealtest tub photographed in-store, with the small line visible.
- Do not write "THIS IS NOT COTTAGE CHEESE" over any named product (rule 6).
- Do not show a tub the video later clears.

### 5b. Order basis. State it on screen once in the open, and as a lower-third on every beat

"Nine tubs, grouped by who owns them, biggest owner first, measured by each company's most recent full-year revenue in its own report." Within an owner, go brand by brand as the list below gives.

| Owner (brands on the list) | Most recent full-year figure, owner's own document | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Loblaw Companies (No Name) | "Retail revenue was $63,903 million" (2025) | S25, Feb 25, 2026 |
| Empire Company / Sobeys (Compliments) | Sales $31,950M, 52 weeks to May 2, 2026 | S26, June 18, 2026 |
| Saputo (Dairyland, Neilson, Baxter) | Revenues $17,551M, year ended March 31, 2026 | S13, June 4, 2026 |
| Agropur Cooperative (Sealtest, Natrel; comparators Northumberland, Island Farms) | "revenues of $8.9 billion for the 2025 fiscal year" | S17, Feb 11, 2026 |
| Gay Lea Foods Co-operative (Nordica) | "record sales of $1.3 billion" (fiscal 2025) | S22 |

On-screen caveat, spoken once: the fiscal years end on different dates, and the figures are whole-company revenue, not cottage cheese sales. No cottage-cheese market share was found at tier (a)/(b)/(d) (U1). That is why "biggest brand first" cannot be documented and owner size is used instead.

Walmart (Great Value) would rank first by this basis. It is excluded because walmart.ca blocked every request, so no Canadian Great Value cottage cheese label or price could be opened (U2). Say nothing about Great Value on air.

### 5c. Eligibility rule for an avoid slot

A tub qualifies only if all four of these are opened and dated:
1. Its name as printed, from a tub photograph or the maker's page.
2. Its full ingredient line from the maker's or a retailer's own page.
3. Its owner from a tier (a) page.
4. A retailer price.

The avoid case must rest on at least one of:
- a name outside the standard ("product", "artisan-style");
- the ingredient line compared with the 7.4.5 list, or with the same owner's other tubs;
- the same recipe sold under several names or at several prices.

Never on what an ingredient does to a body.

### 5d. The jobs in every avoid beat (fixed order)

1. Bridge, with the owner lower-third (10–15 w).
2. Recognition: where you see it, one dated company fact (25–40 w).
3. Owner, with the revenue or member count from 5b (25–40 w).
4. What the front says: the name and the M.F. figure, read slowly (20–40 w).
5. What the back says: the ingredient line read verbatim, then compared with the 7.4.5 list, or with a sibling tub, as a list and never as a judgment (60–120 w).
6. Price: banner, place, date, pack, price, $/100 g (25–35 w).
7. Concession: what is legal, normal, or genuinely good about it (15–30 w).
8. Closer: one line that names the gap between front and back (10–20 w).

### 5e. Word budgets and positions

| # | Beat | Words | Cumulative words at start | Start, % of words (≈ % chars) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Cold open | 150 | 0 | 0.0% |
| 2 | "What the law calls cottage cheese" (60 seconds) | 210 | 150 | 4.0% |
| 3 | #1 No Name (Loblaw) | 320 | 360 | 9.5% |
| 4 | #2 Compliments (Sobeys) | 290 | 680 | 18.0% |
| 5 | #3 Dairyland (Saputo) | 280 | 970 | 25.7% |
| 6 | #4 Neilson (Saputo) | 200 | 1,250 | 33.1% |
| 7 | #5 Baxter (Saputo) | 230 | 1,450 | 38.4% |
| 8 | #6 Sealtest (Agropur): the word "product" | 380 | 1,680 | 44.5% |

| 10 | Mid-list ask (the only mid-list ask) | 50 | 2,310 | 61.2% |

The ask starts at word 2,310 of 3,775, which is 61.2%. At an even 5.95 chars/word that is about 13,745 of 22,460 characters, also 61.2%. Measure the draft in characters before recording. If the ask falls outside 61–63% of characters, move words between #1 and #9, not into the ask.

### 5f. Cold open (150 w). Opens on a dated fact, five moves

1. Dated fact, first sentence: "On August 11th, 2026, the dairy farmers' co-op that makes Nordica announced more than 200 million dollars for one Toronto plant, and in its own press release, it said the money would 'help address the national cottage cheese shortage.'" (S23). Do not quote the release's next sentence, which uses health language.
2. The national number: Canadian plants made 23,474 tonnes of cottage cheese in 2023 and 28,139 tonnes in 2024, "according to Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada's cheese profile, built on Statistics Canada data" (S7). That is +19.9%, computed. Say "about a fifth more in one year".
3. The hook word, cheese-video style: "Everyone is buying it. Almost nobody is reading one small word on some of the tubs. The word is product."
4. Method and count: "We read the federal standard, every maker's own page, and the shelf prices at three Canadian grocers on September 30th. Nine tubs to put back, grouped by who owns them, biggest owner first, by each company's own revenue figures. Then three that are exactly what the law describes."
5. Tagline, first of two uses: "Because the truth is not always on the menu."

No ask. No health word. No sodium, protein or "high-protein" reference, even though the shortage story invites one.

### 5g. "What the law calls cottage cheese" (210 w)

Quote S2 7.4.5 in plain speech, keeping the words "discrete curd particles", "skim milk", "harmless acid-producing bacterial cultures", "not more than 80% moisture", and read the "may contain" list aloud: milk, cream, milk powder, salt, added lactose, relishes, fruits, vegetables.

Then 7.4.6: "creamed" means "not less than 4% milk fat".

Then S1 B.08.002.3(k),(l): the milk fat percentage must be on the front.

Then the key sentence: "The standard has two names in it. Cottage cheese. Creamed cottage cheese. Search both federal documents for the words 'cottage cheese product' and you get zero results." (S1, S2 full-text searches, 30 Sep 2026.)

Then S4, as neutral fact: Health Canada keeps a separate list of stabilizers allowed in cottage cheese, "if used singly, the amount not to exceed 5,000 ppm". Name only the ones confirmed there: carrageenan, carob bean gum, guar gum, gelatin, alginates, karaya gum, mono- and diglycerides. Say nothing about what they do in a body.

Then S5: "milk ingredients" on a label is a class name covering "butter, buttermilk, butter oil, milk fat, cream, milk, partly skimmed milk and skim milk".

Close the explainer with the ruler the video uses: "So we hold every tub to two things. The name on the front. And the list on the back, against the list in the law."

### 5h. The nine avoid beats, with the documented material each turns on

#1 No Name (Loblaw), 320 w.
- Recognition: yellow-and-black, "debuted with 16 products in yellow-and-black packaging" (S28, Canadian Grocer, Rebecca Harris, May 14, 2026). The year needs confirming (U10).
- Owner: Loblaw, $63,903M in 2025 (S25).
- Front: "Cottage Cheese", "2% M.F. Cottage Cheese", "1% M.F. Light Cottage Cheese" (S29 listing names; photograph the tubs).
- Back: three tubs, three different lines at the same store on the same day (S29):
  - 500 g "Cottage Cheese": "Milk Ingredients, Salt, Guar Gum, Carrageenan, Carob Bean Gum, Sugars (dextrose), Lactic Acid, Microbial Enzyme, Bacterial Culture, Carbon Dioxide."
  - 750 g 2%: "Milk Ingredients, Salt, Citric Acid, Guar Gum, Mono- And Diglycerides, Xanthan Gum, Carob Bean Gum, Bacterial Culture, Microbial Enzyme, Carbon Dioxide."
  - 500 g 1% Light: the 750 g line plus "Lactase Enzyme".
- The retailer page does not name the dairy that makes any of them. Concession: that is normal and lawful for a store brand.
- Price: Real Canadian Superstore, Argentia Rd, Mississauga, 30 Sep 2026: 500 g $4.50 = $0.90/100 g; 750 g $6.79 = $0.91/100 g. This is the cheapest per 100 g of anything found.
- Closer: the cheapest tub in the store is three different recipes under one yellow label.

#2 Compliments (Sobeys), 290 w.
- Owner: Sobeys "Own Brands", "Since the launch of the Compliments brand in 2005" (S27); Empire $31,950M FY2026 (S26).
- Back: on Sobeys' own Voilà site, 30 Sep 2026, two different "Compliments 2% Cottage Cheese 500 g" listings (S30):
  - 484716EA, $5.15: "Milk Ingredients, Salt, Carob Bean Gum, Guar Gum, Mono- And Diglycerides, Carrageenan, Xanthan Gum, Polysorbate 80, Bacterial Culture, Microbial Enzyme, Carbon Dioxide".
  - 440855EA, $5.59: "Milk Ingredients, Salt, Citric Acid, Guar Gum, Mono- And Diglycerides, Xanthan Gum, Carob Bean Gum, Bacterial Culture, Microbial Enzyme, Carbon Dioxide".
- Same brand, same size, same site, two lists, 44 cents apart ($1.03 vs $1.12 /100 g).
- Fat-Free 0.3% (484720EA) lists "Tapioca Starch" and "Colour".
- The retailer does not name the maker.
- Do not mention the "No Salt Added" SKU (sodium).
- Concession: both lists are legal. The shopper just cannot tell which tub is which from the front.
- Closer: at Sobeys, "Compliments 2%" is a name, not a recipe.

#3 Dairyland (Saputo), 280 w.
- Owner: © Saputo Inc. (S10); Saputo $17,551M FY2026 (S13).
- The company line, attributed: "Dairyland offers consumers a variety of high-quality products manufactured in Canada, using milk from Canadian farms." (S10)
- Back, 2% (S10, S31): "Milk ingredients, Salt, Locust bean gum, Guar gum, Mono and diglycerides, Carrageenan, Xanthan gum, Polysorbate 80, Bacterial culture, Microbial enzyme, Carbon dioxide (to maintain product freshness)." That is eleven items. Only milk ingredients and salt are named in 7.4.5's own paragraph; the culture is named in its opening definition.
- Set up the payoff without giving it away: "Hold on to this brand. It comes back in the good list."
- Price: Save-On-Foods Langley BC, 30 Sep 2026 (record modified 29 Sep): 500 g $5.49 = $1.10/100 g.
- Do not use the "Most Trusted" award line; the BrandSpark methodology was not opened (U8). Do not use any of Dairyland's health copy.
- Closer: Canadian milk, a Montreal owner, and eleven items on the back.

#4 Neilson (Saputo), 200 w.
- Recognition: Ontario.
- Owner: © Saputo Inc. (S11).
- The same header sentence as Dairyland, word for word except the brand name (S10 vs S11). Read both on screen side by side.
- Back, 2% (S11, S29): "Milk ingredients, Salt, Guar gum, Carrageenan, Mono- and diglycerides, Locust bean gum, Xanthan gum, Polysorbate 80, Bacterial culture, Microbial enzyme*, Carbon dioxide (to maintain product freshness). *May be present." The same eleven items as Dairyland, in a different order.
- Price: Superstore Mississauga $5.79/500 g SPECIAL = $1.16/100 g; Voilà $4.99 = $1.00/100 g.
- Closer: different province, different logo, same eleven items.

#5 Baxter (Saputo), 230 w.
- Owner: Saputo lists Baxter among its Canadian brands (S12). No other Baxter history is verified; U5.
- Back: Voilà 2% (76025EA) reads "Milk Ingredients, Salt, Locust Bean Gum, Guar Gum, Mono And Diglycerides, Carrageenan, Xanthan Gum, Polysorbate 80, Bacterial Culture, Microbial Enzyme, Carbon Dioxide (To Maintain Product Freshness)". That is Dairyland's line, item for item and in the same order (S30).
- Price, same site, same day, no region selected: Baxter 2% 500 g $7.29 = $1.46/100 g; Neilson 2% 500 g $4.99 = $1.00/100 g. That is 46% more per 100 g for the same eleven items under another Saputo name.
- Concession, required: Voilà did not display a region, and Baxter is an Atlantic brand, so regional pricing may explain the gap. Check a Sobeys flyer or Voilà with an Atlantic postal code before air (U6).
- Closer: three names, one owner, one list, and the price moves with the logo.

#6 Sealtest (Agropur), 380 w. The centrepiece, and the longest beat.
- Recognition: Ontario and Quebec (S14: "This product is available in Ontario, Quebec").
- Owner: "SEALTEST is a registered trademark of Unilever PLC. Used under licence in Canada by Agropur Cooperative." (S14). Agropur has 2,700 members and $8.9B in 2025 revenue (S17). "100% owned by dairy farmers" (S14, company says).
- Front: Sealtest's own pages name all three tubs "Cottage Cheese Product"; in French, "Produit de Fromage Cottage 2%" (S14). The 2% tub photographed on Voilà reads "COTTAGE CHEESE" in large letters and "LOW FAT COTTAGE CHEESE PRODUCT" underneath (S30 image, saved img/sealtest2.jpg).
- The standard names "cottage cheese" and "creamed cottage cheese" only (S2). "Cottage cheese product" appears in neither federal document (S1, S2).
- Back, 1% (S14): "Skim milk, Ultrafiltered skim milk, Sugars (lactose), Cream, Skim milk powder, Salt, Cultured skim milk, Bacterial culture, Calcium chloride, Chymosin B, Carrageenan, Guar gum, Mono- and diglycerides, Xanthan gum, Citric acid, Lactic acid, Carbon dioxide."
- The twist: the same co-op's Northumberland 1% line on Voilà is word for word the Sealtest 1% line (S30). The Northumberland 2% tub front reads "COTTAGE CHEESE", with no "product" (S30 image, img/northumberland2.jpg).
- Agropur's pages do not say why one is "product" and the other is not. Say so plainly, and add verbatim: "We're not saying either label breaks a rule. We're saying the only place the difference shows up is one small word."
- Also say that the Dairy Farmers of Canada catalogue lists the names the other way round (S32), so labels change and viewers should read their own tub.
- Price: Voilà, 30 Sep 2026, 500 g $5.19 = $1.04/100 g.
- Before air, send Agropur a written question asking why the name differs (U4).
- Closer: "The front says cottage. The small print says product. Read the small print."

#7 Natrel "Artisan-Style" (Agropur), 250 w.
- Owner: Natrel is an Agropur brand (S16).
- Front: "ARTISAN-STYLE / LOW FAT COTTAGE CHEESE", 2% (S30 image, img/natrel_artisan2.jpg).
- What the word means, CFIA (S6), verbatim: "The claim 'artisan made' describes products that are manufactured in small batches... a significant portion of manual labour and a limited use of automated machines" and "The claim 'artisan-style' may be used to describe a food prepared in the style of an 'artisan' food. In this case, it is a best practice to qualify the claim 'artisan-style' to describe how it is different from an 'artisan' food."
- Back, 2% (S30): "Skim milk, Cream, Ultrafiltered skim milk, Lactose, Skim milk powder, Salt, Guar gum, Mono and diglycerides, Xanthan gum, Carrageenan, citric acid, Cultured skim milk, Bacterial culture, Lactic acid, Calcium chloride, Microbial enzyme, Carbon dioxide." That is 17 items.
- Price: Voilà $5.29/500 g = $1.06/100 g.
- Concession: "artisan-style" is a permitted phrase. It is not the same claim as "artisan made".
- Do not say or imply that the tub fails CFIA's best practice; legal review U9.
- Closer: seventeen items and the word "artisan", from one of the largest co-ops in North America (S7's wording, attributed to AAFC).

Mid-list ask (50 w), at 61–63% of characters. Keep the cheese-video credibility clause and put one ask in it: "If this is the kind of label reading you want before your next grocery run, subscribe. Canadian Counter reads Canadian labels, standards and fine print every week, from the source. Now, the two tubs from the co-op this channel praised in February."

#8 Nordica Fat-Free (Gay Lea), 270 w.
- Recognition: "1973 Nordica Cottage Cheese is introduced" (S20). "Nordica Cottage Cheese was the standout performer" of fiscal 2025 (S22).
- Owner: "1,200 dairy farmer members in Ontario and Manitoba" (S21); "record sales of $1.3 billion" (S22).
- The February reversal line from 4g (25 w).
- Back (S18, S29): Nordica 2% reads "Milk Ingredients, Salt, Citric Acid, Guar Gum, Mono- And Diglycerides, Xanthan Gum, Locust Bean Gum, Bacterial Culture, Microbial Enzyme, Carbon Dioxide, Vitamin D3*". The Fat-Free tub from the same co-op reads "Modified Milk Ingredients, Salt, Modified Corn Starch, Citric Acid, Titanium Dioxide, Guar Gum, Mono And Diglycerides, Carrageenan, Bacterial Culture, Microbial Enzyme, Carbon Dioxide, Vitamin A Palmitate*, Vitamin D3*".
- Explain the class name from S5: "modified milk ingredients" is being renamed "milk-derived ingredients" by January 1, 2030, and covers items like "ultrafiltered milk, whey... proteins derived from milk".
- Read titanium dioxide as a word on the list only. No comment of any kind.
- Price: Superstore Mississauga $5.49/500 g = $1.10/100 g (the same price as Nordica 2%).
- Concession: every item is declared, and the 2%, 1% and 4% tubs use the class name "milk ingredients".
- Closer: the same co-op, the same price, a different first word.

#9 Nordica Smooth (Gay Lea), 240 w.
- Front, from Gay Lea's own pack image: "1.7% M.F. COTTAGE CHEESE PRODUCT / PRODUIT AU FROMAGE COTTAGE 1.7% M.G." with the blue cow logo (S19). The image was uploaded in 2017, so photograph a current pack (U7).
- The standard's first words: "in the form of discrete curd particles" (S2). This one is sold as "Smooth".
- Back, Smooth Vanilla Bean (S18), read verbatim: "Milk ingredients, sugar, water, milk protein concentrate, rice starch, natural flavour, bacterial culture, pectin, vanilla beans, lemon juice concentrate, guar gum, xanthan gum, carrageenan, locust bean gum, microbial enzyme." Say nothing about sugar beyond reading the word.
- Price: not found at any retailer opened (U7). The beat cannot be recorded until one is captured.
- Concession: the pack says "product" on its face, and the blue cow says the milk is Canadian (S32 definition).
- Closer: two co-ops, two tubs, the same small word, and it is on the front for anyone who reads it.

### 5i. The turn (70 w) and disclosure

"So where's the cottage cheese that reads like the law? It exists. Two of them come from companies already on this list. We have no commercial relationship with any company named in this video, and none of them knew this video was being made."

The disclosure is spoken once, here only.

### 5j. The three "actually real" picks

The eligibility test, said on air once: the name is "cottage cheese", and the ingredient line holds only skim milk and bacterial culture, the two things 7.4.5's first sentence names, plus at most a coagulating enzyme.

Enzyme caveat for the writer: "microbial enzyme" is not in the 7.4.5 paragraph either. Do not claim the dry curd tubs match the paragraph "exactly". Say "skim milk, culture, and an enzyme — three items".

R1 Dairyland Dry Curd (Saputo), 190 w.
- "Skim milk, Bacterial culture, Microbial enzyme." (S10, S30, S31).
- Payoff: the same company as #3–#5, the same logo as #3.
- Price, stated honestly:
  - Voilà $7.49/600 g = $1.25/100 g.
  - Save-On-Foods Langley $9.19/600 g = $1.53/100 g.
  - The 2% Dairyland tub at the same Save-On store: $1.10/100 g.
- Concession, required: the short list costs more per 100 g, not less. The U.S. videos' "cheapest is cleanest" line does not hold in these Canadian prices.
- The tub says "dry curd"; the Dairyland page adds nothing verifiable about the process. Make no texture claim.
- Closer: Saputo makes the three-item tub too; it sits one shelf over.

R2 Compliments Dry Curd 0.4% (Sobeys), 150 w.
- "Skim milk, Bacterial culture, Microbial enzyme" (S30).
- Price: Voilà $7.99/600 g = $1.33/100 g.
- Concession: the maker is not named on the page.
- Closer: the same house brand as #2, and this time the recipe is the name.

R3 M-C Dairy pressed cottage cheese (Toronto), 180 w.
- Superstore listing: "Cottage Cheese Pressed 0.8 % M.F.", ingredients "Pasteurized Skim Milk, Bacterial Culture." (S29). That is two items, no enzyme.
- Maker: "M-C Dairy & Future Bakery", Toronto, whose organic pressed product is listed "Proudly made in Ontario" on the Ontario Made site (S33). That claim is attributed and covers the organic product only.
- Price: Superstore Mississauga $8.79/500 g = $1.76/100 g; Voilà $10.29/500 g = $2.06/100 g. It is the most expensive per 100 g of the three. Say so.
- Farm Boy's pressed tub is also on Voilà ($8.49/500 g = $1.70), but its ingredient line was not captured (U14), so it cannot be named as a pick.
- Closer: the shortest list in the dairy case, two items, from a Toronto dairy.

### 5k. How to protect yourself (190 w). Four numbered steps: "First," "Second," "Third," "Fourth"

- First, read the name on the front against the federal standard. It should say "cottage cheese", or "creamed cottage cheese" at 4% or more. Anything else is a different food, "product" included. The milk fat number beside it is required by law (S1, S2).
- Second, read the ingredient line all the way through. The standard's own list is skim milk and cultures, and it may add milk, cream, milk powder, salt and lactose. Everything else on the line is allowed through a separate list. Count them. Do not guess what they do.
- Third, look for the owner, the plant and the milk. The label must name the company responsible. The blue cow logo means, in Dairy Farmers of Canada's words, "made with 100% Canadian milk and milk ingredients" (S32). A regional logo does not tell you the owner; this video's lower-thirds do.
- Fourth, compare price per 100 grams on the shelf tag, never per tub. The short-list tubs cost more per 100 g in every store checked on September 30th.

### 5l. Moral, comment ask, tagline (80 w)

Moral: "Nine tubs, five owners, one lesson. The law already tells you what cottage cheese is. The companies decide how loudly the label says it."

Comment ask, framed as data, cheese-video style: "Open your fridge, read the small words under the brand, and tell us in the comments exactly what your tub says: cottage cheese, creamed cottage cheese, or cottage cheese product, and what you paid."

Tagline, second and last use: "Because the truth is not always on the menu."

### 5m. Disclaimer (45 w), spoken last and shown on screen

"Everything in this video comes from the federal food standards, company websites and annual reports, and retailer websites checked on September 30th, 2026. Labels, recipes and prices change and vary by store and region. Read the tub in your hand. Not sponsored."

### 5n. Vocabulary rules for the body

- Never use: healthy, clean, cleaner, protein (except inside a product name read verbatim), sodium, salt intake, calories, gut, probiotic, live culture benefit, filler, cheap, chemical, synthetic, engineered, industrial, lab-made, ultra-processed, "no nutritional value", safe, deadly, toxic, inflammation.
- Read every ingredient line verbatim, then compare it only with (a) the 7.4.5 list or (b) a sibling tub.
- Every price carries banner, city or "online", date, pack and $/100 g.
- Every company claim carries "the company says" or "on its own site".
- No "we tasted", no texture words, except Island Farms' own "larger, more distinct curds", attributed, if used at all.
- No U.S. brand or U.S. regulation (21 CFR) is named except as "in the U.S." context, and none is needed.

# Cottage cheese in Canada: the legal definition, and what a "real cottage cheese" test can lawfully rest on

**Compiled:** 30 Sep 2026. Every source below was opened on 30 Sep 2026 unless the entry says otherwise.
**Scope:** Canadian federal law and guidance only. There is no nutrition, health, safety or additive-safety commentary. Recalls and microbiological rules appear only in Appendix A, marked DO NOT USE ON AIR.
**Tiers:** (a) primary / government / company's own site; (b) named outlet with byline and date; (c) flag only; (d) named survey with methodology.

---

## 0. Headline findings (tier (a))

| # | Finding | Source (tier a) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Sections B.08.051 and B.08.052 of the Food and Drug Regulations (FDR), which held the cottage cheese standards, are repealed.** Justice Laws marks both "[Repealed, SOR/2024-244, s. 57]". The old text was in force "from 2010-06-17 to 2024-12-17" (B.08.051) and "from 2006-03-22 to 2024-12-17" (B.08.052). | FDR full text, https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/FullText.html (current to 2026-09-21, last amended 2026-06-17); previous versions: https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/section-B.08.051-20100617.html and https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/section-B.08.052-20060322.html |
| 2 | **The current legal definition** is s. **7.4.5 "Cottage Cheese"** and s. **7.4.6 "Creamed Cottage Cheese"** in Volume 7 of the CFIA's *Canadian Food Compositional Standards* (CFCS). The FDR incorporates this document by reference as the "Food Compositional Standards Document" (FDR B.01.001(1), B.01.002, B.01.042). | CFCS, https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/acts-and-regulations/list-acts-and-regulations/documents-incorporated-reference/canadian-food-compositional-standards-0 (page "Date modified: 2025-05-01") |
| 3 | The SFCR-side document (*Canadian Standards of Identity, Volume 1 – Dairy Products*) points back to the CFCS. s. 28: "Cottage Cheese is the food that meets the standard set out in section 7.4.5 of Volume 7 of the FCSD." s. 29 does the same for creamed cottage cheese and s. 7.4.6. | CSI Vol. 1, https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/acts-and-regulations/list-acts-and-regulations/documents-incorporated-reference/canadian-standards-identity-volume-1 ("Last amended on 2024-12-18") |
| 4 | Food additives (stabilizers, calcium chloride, acids, enzymes) **no longer appear in the standard itself**. They are now permitted through Health Canada's Lists of Permitted Food Additives. The old B.08.051(c) cap, "not more than 0.5 per cent stabilizing agent", now appears in those lists as 5,000 ppm per food entry. This came from a summarising fetch tool and is **not verbatim-verified** (see s. 4). | Gazette SOR/2024-244 RIAS, https://gazette.gc.ca/rp-pr/p2/2024/2024-12-18/html/sor-dors244-eng.html; Health Canada List 4 (see s. 4) |
| 5 | Cottage cheese and creamed cottage cheese are **excluded from the general "(naming the variety) cheese" standard** (CFCS 7.4.2, formerly FDR B.08.033). They are also exempt from the **moisture %** declaration (FDR B.08.032(1); SFCR 248(a)) and from the firmness and ripening descriptors (SFCR 249(2)(j),(k)). The **milk-fat %** must still appear on the principal display panel (FDR B.08.002.3(k),(l); SFCR 248(b)). | FDR; SFCR, https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/SOR-2018-108/FullText.html (current to 2026-09-21) |
| 6 | **"Modified milk ingredients" is being renamed "milk-derived ingredients".** The CFIA changed the class names on 11 Feb 2026 and set a transition to **1 Jan 2030**. During the transition "regulated parties may comply with either the previous or new labelling requirements." | CFIA notice, https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/notice-2026-02-11 |
| 7 | No federal regulation or CFIA document opened today defines **"cottage cheese product"**, "dry curd cottage cheese" or "whipped cottage cheese". | Search of the CFCS, CSI Vol. 1, FDR and SFCR texts (see s. 6) |

---

## 1. The current standard, verbatim (tier a)

### 1.1 CFCS Volume 7, s. 7.4.5 and 7.4.6 (in force since SOR/2024-244; page modified 2025-05-01)
URL: https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/acts-and-regulations/list-acts-and-regulations/documents-incorporated-reference/canadian-food-compositional-standards-0

> "7.4.5 **Cottage Cheese** is the product, in the form of discrete curd particles, prepared from skim milk, evaporated skim milk or skim milk powder and harmless acid-producing bacterial cultures;
> (a) contains not more than 80% moisture; and
> (b) may contain
> (i) milk,
> (ii) cream,
> (iii) milk powder,
> (iv) salt,
> (v) added lactose,
> (vi) relishes,
> (vii) fruits, and
> (viii) vegetables."

> "7.4.6 **Creamed Cottage Cheese** is cottage cheese containing cream or a mixture of cream with milk or skim milk, or both, in such quantity that the final product and contains
> (a) not less than 4% milk fat; and
> (b) not more than 80% moisture."

*Note: the words "the final product and contains" in 7.4.6 are quoted exactly as published. The draft CFCS used the same wording: https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/transparency/consultations-and-engagement/canadian-food-compositional-standards (archived draft, which maps "[B.08.052] 7.4.6").*

**Limits at a glance (from the text above):**

| Item | Cottage cheese (7.4.5) | Creamed cottage cheese (7.4.6) |
|---|---|---|
| Physical form | "discrete curd particles" | is "cottage cheese" (so the same) |
| Required base | "skim milk, evaporated skim milk or skim milk powder and harmless acid-producing bacterial cultures" | cottage cheese plus "cream or a mixture of cream with milk or skim milk, or both" |
| Moisture | "not more than 80%" | "not more than 80%" |
| Milk fat | **no minimum or maximum stated** | "not less than 4%" |
| Optional ingredients named | milk, cream, milk powder, salt, added lactose, relishes, fruits, vegetables | (none listed beyond the cottage cheese base and the cream mixture) |
| Food additives | not in the standard; governed by Health Canada's Lists (s. 4) | same |

Related definition, CFCS 7.4.0: "pickles and relishes mean foods that comply with the standard set out in section 10.5.2 of Volume 10". "Milk" is defined at 7.1.1. The CFIA dairy page quotes it: "Milk, unless otherwise designated, refers to cow's milk [7.1.1, Canadian Food Compositional Standards (CFCS)]" (https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/dairy).

### 1.2 How the FDR makes the CFCS binding (current FDR, current to 2026-09-21)
URL: https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/FullText.html

- **B.01.001(1), definition:** "Food Compositional Standards Document means the document entitled Canadian Food Compositional Standards, prepared by the Canadian Food Inspection Agency and published on its website, as amended from time to time".
- **B.01.002:** "The Food Compositional Standards Document sets out the standards of composition, strength, potency, purity, quality or other property of the foods that are referred to in that document." (SOR/79-752, s. 1; SOR/2024-244, s. 4)
- **B.01.042:** "If a standard for a food is set out in the Food Compositional Standards Document: (a) the food must contain only the ingredients set out in the standard for the food; (b) the food must contain each ingredient in a quantity within any limits set out in the standard for that ingredient; (c) the food must be labelled and advertised in accordance with any requirements set out in the standard; and (d) the food must comply with any other requirements set out in the standard in relation to the composition, strength, potency, purity, quality or other property of the food." (SOR/2024-244, s. 13)
- **B.01.043:** "Despite paragraph B.01.042(a), if a substance — other than a supplemental ingredient — is required or permitted by a provision of these Regulations in a food for which a standard is set out in the Food Compositional Standards Document, that food must contain the required substance and may contain the permitted substance."
- **B.01.001(1), "common name":** "(a) the name of the food printed in boldface type, but not in italics, in a section of the Food Compositional Standards Document; (b) the name of the food printed in boldface type, but not in italics, in a section of the Standards of Identity Document, as defined in section 1 of the Safe Food for Canadians Regulations; or (c) in any other case, the name by which it is generally known or a name that is not generic and that describes the food".
- **B.01.001(1), "unstandardized":** "in relation to a food, means that a standard is not set out in the Food Compositional Standards Document for that food".
- **B.01.006(1):** "The common name of the food shall be shown on the principal display panel."

### 1.3 Remaining FDR Division 8 provisions that name cottage cheese
- **B.08.002.3:** "The percentage of milk fat contained in the following dairy products must be shown on the principal display panel followed by the words 'milk fat' or the abbreviation 'B.F.' or 'M.F.': … (k) cottage cheese; and (l) creamed cottage cheese." (SOR/2024-244, s. 48)
- **B.08.032(1):** "Cheeses, other than cottage cheese and creamed cottage cheese, must be labelled to show on the principal display panel a statement of the percentage of milk fat in the food followed by the words 'milk fat' or the abbreviation 'B.F.' or 'M.F.', and the percentage of moisture in the food followed by the word 'moisture' or 'water'."
- **B.08.032(2):** "Subject to subsection B.01.301(1), no person shall make any direct or indirect reference on the label of a food referred to in subsection (1) to the milk fat content or moisture content of the food except as required by subsection (1)." *(Applies to cheeses in (1), and therefore not to cottage cheese.)*
- **B.08.053:** "All dairy products used in the preparation of cottage cheese shall be from a pasteurized source." *On air, quote this as a rule that exists. Do not present it as a safety point (house rule 1).*
- **B.08.002:** "Except as provided in these Regulations, a dairy product that contains a fat other than milk fat is adulterated." *"Adulterated" is a legal term of art here. On air, say "the Regulations do not allow a dairy product to contain fat other than milk fat, except where they say otherwise". Do not use the word to describe any product.*
- **B.08.030(1), "pasteurized source"** (cheese) is defined by reference to official method MFO-3. It is technical; do not use on air.

### 1.4 The repealed FDR text, for "what changed" (tier a, Justice Laws previous versions)

**B.08.051, version 2010-06-17 to 2024-12-17**, https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/section-B.08.051-20100617.html:
> "B.08.051 [S]. Cottage Cheese
> (a) shall be the product, in the form of discrete curd particles, prepared from skim milk, evaporated skim milk or skim milk powder and harmless acid-producing bacterial cultures;
> (b) shall contain not more than 80 per cent moisture;
> (c) may contain not more than 0.5 per cent stabilizing agent; and
> (d) may contain
> (i) milk, (ii) cream, (iii) milk powder, (iv) rennet derived from aqueous extracts from the fourth stomach of calves, kids or lambs, (v) a milk coagulating enzyme derived from Rhizomucor miehei (Cooney and Emerson) (previous name: Mucor miehei (Cooney and Emerson)), from Mucor pusillus Lindt by pure culture fermentation process or from Aspergillus oryzae RET-1 (pBoel777), in an amount consistent with good manufacturing practice, (vi) chymosin A derived from Escherichia coli K-12, GE81 (pPFZ87A), in an amount consistent with good manufacturing practice, (vi.1) chymosin B derived from Aspergillus niger var. awamori, GCC0349 (pGAMpR) or from Kluyveromyces marxianus var. lactis, DS1182 (pKS105), in an amount consistent with good manufacturing practice, (vi.2) pepsin derived from glandular layer of porcine stomach, (vii) salt, (viii) calcium chloride, (ix) added lactose, (x) pH adjusting agents, (xi) relishes, (xii) fruits, (xiii) vegetables, and (xiv) carbon dioxide."

**B.08.052, version 2006-03-22 to 2024-12-17**, https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/section-B.08.052-20060322.html:
> "B.08.052 [S]. Creamed Cottage Cheese shall be cottage cheese containing cream or a mixture of cream with milk or skim milk, or both, in such quantity that the final product shall contain (a) not less than four per cent milk fat; and (b) not more than 80 per cent moisture and may contain emulsifying, gelling, stabilizing and thickening agents."

**Old B.01.042 (2006-03-22 to 2024-12-17)**, https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/section-B.01.042-20060322.html: "Where a standard for a food is prescribed in this Part, (a) the food shall contain only the ingredients included in the standard for the food; (b) each ingredient shall be incorporated in the food in a quantity within any limits prescribed for that ingredient; …"

**What moved where (the government's explanation).** SOR/2024-244 was registered 29 Nov 2024 and published in *Canada Gazette* Part II, Vol. 158, No. 26 (18 Dec 2024). Its s. 172 says: "These Regulations come into force on the day on which they are published in the Canada Gazette, Part II." The Regulatory Impact Analysis Statement says:
> "these Regulations will move the elements of the food compositional standards that do not relate to health and safety from the FDR to a document entitled Canadian Food Compositional Standards, which will be incorporated by reference into the FDR."
> "With certain exceptions, the food compositional standards set out in the Canadian Food Compositional Standards will not refer to food additives."
> "The symbol '[S]' will no longer appear in the FDR to indicate a food for which a standard is prescribed".
URL: https://gazette.gc.ca/rp-pr/p2/2024/2024-12-18/html/sor-dors244-eng.html

**Side-by-side (our comparison of the two tier (a) texts above; the comparison itself is analysis):**

| Element | Old B.08.051 (to 2024-12-17) | CFCS 7.4.5 (now) | Where it went |
|---|---|---|---|
| Base: skim milk / evaporated skim milk / skim milk powder + acid-producing cultures | yes | yes, same words | unchanged |
| Moisture ≤ 80% | yes | yes | unchanged |
| "not more than 0.5 per cent stabilizing agent" | yes, (c) | not in standard | Health Canada List 4 (5,000 ppm entries; s. 4) |
| Milk, cream, milk powder, salt, added lactose, relishes, fruits, vegetables | yes | yes | unchanged |
| Rennet, chymosin A/B, pepsin, milk-coagulating enzymes | yes | not in standard | Health Canada List 5, Food Enzymes (s. 4) |
| Calcium chloride | yes | not in standard | Health Canada List 6, Firming Agents (s. 4) |
| pH adjusting agents | yes | not in standard | Health Canada List 10, Acidity Regulators (s. 4) |
| Carbon dioxide | yes | not in standard | not verified (UNVERIFIED) |
| Creamed: ≥4% milk fat, ≤80% moisture | yes | yes | unchanged |
| Creamed: "may contain emulsifying, gelling, stabilizing and thickening agents" | yes | not in standard | Health Canada List 4 (s. 4) |

## 2. The general cheese standard and whether cottage cheese is exempt (tier a)

- **CFCS 7.4.2** (formerly FDR B.08.033) applies to "(Naming the variety) Cheese, other than cheddar cheese, cream cheese, whey cheese, … cold-pack cheese food with (naming the added ingredients), **cottage cheese and creamed cottage cheese**". The same exclusion was in B.08.033(1) (version 2022-07-20 to 2024-12-17, https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/section-B.08.033-20220720.html).
- As a result, the variety-cheese requirements do **not** apply to cottage cheese. These include being "made by coagulating milk … with the aid of bacteria to form a curd and forming the curd into a homogeneous mass", the casein-to-protein minimums (63% / 83% / 95%), and "a whey protein to casein ratio that does not exceed the whey protein to casein ratio of milk". Cottage cheese has its own standard at 7.4.5.
- The processed-cheese, cold-pack and cheese-food standards (CFCS 7.4.11 to 7.4.20) are each made from cheese "other than cream cheese, cottage cheese or whey cheese" (CFCS text above).
- **Cheddar** (CFCS 7.4.3, formerly B.08.034, https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/C.R.C.,_c._870/section-B.08.034-20220720.html) is a separate standard and has no bearing on cottage cheese.
- **CSI Vol. 1 s. 11(2)** also carves out "(q) cottage cheese; and (r) creamed cottage cheese" from "(Naming the variety) Cheese". **s. 30** allows higher moisture and lower milk fat for cheeses under ss. 13 to 29, which includes cottage cheese (28) and creamed cottage cheese (29), "if (a) the characteristic flavour and texture of the cheese are maintained in the modified cheese; and (b) the name of the cheese includes descriptive terminology that meets the requirements of the Food and Drugs Act and the FDR with respect to claims and statements." *(This is a nutrient-claim route. House rule: we do not discuss it on air beyond noting that it exists.)*

## 3. Labelling rules specific to cottage cheese (tier a)

| Requirement | Applies to cottage cheese? | Legal text / source |
|---|---|---|
| Common name on the principal display panel | Yes | FDR B.01.006(1); SFCR 218(1)(a) (cited by the CFIA dairy page) |
| Milk-fat % on the PDP, followed by "milk fat", "B.F." or "M.F." | **Yes** | FDR B.08.002.3(k),(l); SFCR 248(b): "in the case of cheese, cheese curd and evaporated partly skimmed milk or concentrated partly skimmed milk, the percentage of milk fat" |
| Moisture % | **No** | SFCR 248(a): "in the case of cheese, except cottage cheese and creamed cottage cheese, and in the case of cheese curd, the percentage of moisture"; FDR B.08.032(1) |
| Relative firmness / ripening descriptor | **No** | SFCR 249(2): "Subsection (1) does not apply in respect of … (j) cottage cheese; (k) creamed cottage cheese" |
| Net quantity unit | By **weight** | *Units of Measurement for the Net Quantity Declaration of Certain Foods*, Table 2 (consumer prepackaged), item 6 "Cottage cheese", weight "applicable", https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/acts-and-regulations/list-acts-and-regulations/documents-incorporated-reference/units-measurement-net-quantity-declaration-certain-foods (modified 2025-10-08) |
| Animal source if not cow | Yes | SFCR 248(c); FDR B.08.028.1, B.08.031 |
| "Product of [country]" if imported | Yes | SFCR 250(1)(a): "an imported prepackaged dairy product" |
| Canadian grade name | **None exists for cottage cheese** | CFIA dairy page: grade names apply to "butter, calorie-reduced butter, dairy spread, light butter or lite butter, whey butter and cheddar cheese" (Canada 1) and "dry milk products" (Canada 1 / Canada 2). https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/dairy |

The CFIA's own table ("Percent (%) milk fat and moisture declarations", same URL, modified 2026-05-28) lists: "Cottage cheese | Milk fat | B.08.002.3(k), FDR" and "Creamed cottage cheese | Milk fat | B.08.002.3(l), FDR".

**SFCR definition of "dairy product" (s. 1):** "means milk or a food that is derived from milk, alone or combined with another food, and that contains no oil and no fat other than that of milk." https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/SOR-2018-108/FullText.html

## 4. Food additives now permitted in cottage cheese (Health Canada Lists of Permitted Food Additives)

**Access caveat (read first).** In this session, `curl` to www.canada.ca failed each time with "HTTP/2 stream … INTERNAL_ERROR" or "Empty reply from server", and the Wayback Machine was unreachable. The pages below were read only through a web-fetch tool that summarises the page. That tool **admitted truncation on List 4** ("NOT SEEN (truncated)" for items after roughly the letter M) and gave inconsistent item lists across three passes. **Treat everything in s. 4 as "seen, not verbatim-verified".** Before air, open each list in a browser and copy the rows. The index page, https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted.html, returned "Date Modified: 2026-04-22".

| List | Entry the tool returned for cottage cheese | Item(s) returned | Version shown |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4. Emulsifying, Gelling, Stabilizing or Thickening Agents, https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted/4-emulsifying-gelling-stabilizing-thickening-agents.html | "Cottage cheese / Stabilizing agent / If used singly, the amount not to exceed 5,000 ppm. If used in any combination with other stabilizing agents, the total combined amount not to exceed 5,000 ppm." Creamed cottage cheese: "Emulsifying, stabilizing or thickening agent" (or "Gelling agent") with a 5,000 ppm single/combined cap | 5,000 ppm entries: A.6 Alginic acid, A.7 Ammonium alginate, A.8 Ammonium carrageenan, C.1 Calcium alginate, C.3 Calcium carrageenan, C.13 Carob bean gum, C.14 Carrageenan, G.1 Gelatin, G.3 Guar gum, K.1 Karaya gum; one pass also gave A.5 Agar. "Good Manufacturing Practice": M.5 Mono- and diglycerides; one pass also gave L.3 Lecithin. C.11 Calcium sulphate: creamed cottage cheese "500 ppm" only. **Items after M not seen** (e.g. xanthan gum, sodium/potassium alginate, pectin, polysorbates, sodium carboxymethyl cellulose). | "Published 2025-10-20; Supersedes 2024-12-18" |
| 5. Food Enzymes, https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted/5-enzymes.html | Cottage cheese is among the foods for C.5 Chymosin, C.6 Chymosin A, C.7 Chymosin B, M.4 Milk coagulating enzyme, P.7 Pepsin. **Lactase (L.1): "Cottage cheese" does NOT appear** in its permitted foods (the tool returned "Bread; White flour; Whole wheat flour", "Lactose-reducing enzyme preparations", flavoured milks, "Milk for use in ice cream mix", "Whey", and "Yogurt" for some sources). | see left | "Date Published: 2026-04-14" |
| 6. Firming Agents, https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted/6-firming-agents.html | C.1 Calcium chloride: "Cottage cheese / Firming agent / Good Manufacturing Practice" | C.1 | 2025-10-20 |
| 10. Acidity Regulators and Acid-Reacting Materials, https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted/10-adjusting-agents.html | "Acidity regulator / Good Manufacturing Practice" | L.1 Lactic acid; C.16 Citric acid; P.1 Phosphoric acid; S.9 Sodium citrate | 2025-10-20 |
| 11. Preservatives, https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted/11-preservatives.html | C.6 Chitosan (as described on the list): "Cottage cheese / Preservative / 1,500 ppm" (the only cottage cheese entry the tool reported) | C.6 | 2025-10-20 |

**Stabilizer quantity limit, stated safely:** 5,000 ppm equals 0.5% by weight (arithmetic). The pre-2024 FDR said "not more than 0.5 per cent stabilizing agent" (tier a, verbatim, s. 1.4). For the current rule, cite List 4 only after verbatim verification.

**Canadian Dairy Commission** (tier a), https://cdc-ccl.ca/en/node/781: "The maximum allowable moisture content for cottage cheese according to Canadian law is 80%. Stabilizing agents (such as polysaccharide gums) can be added to the cottage cheese at levels up to 0.5% to help hold moisture. Creamed cottage cheese may contain additional emulsifying, gelling, stabilizing and thickening agents." *(The page is undated. The rest of it contains nutrition and safety text, which must not be used.)*

**How the FDR lets additives in (tier a, verbatim):** FDR B.16.003 says a food with an additive from the Lists is not adulterated "by reason only that the food additive is in or on it" if it is used within the listed food, purpose and "maximum level of use" (or "the amount that is required to accomplish the purpose for which it is added, in the case where the words 'Good Manufacturing Practice' appear in column 5"). *House rule: describe additives only as "permitted by Health Canada's list for cottage cheese". Never characterise them.*

## 5. Products that do not meet the standard: what they must be called

### 5.1 The statutory rules (tier a, verbatim)
- **Food and Drugs Act s. 6(1)** (https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/acts/F-27/FullText.html, current to 2026-09-21): "Where a standard for a food has been prescribed, no person shall (a) import into Canada, (b) send, convey or receive for conveyance from one province to another, or (c) have in possession for the purpose of sending or conveying from one province to another any article that is intended for sale and that is likely to be mistaken for that food unless the article complies with the prescribed standard." s. 6(3) applies the same test to labelling, packaging, selling or advertising imported or interprovincial articles.
- **Food and Drugs Act s. 5(1):** "No person shall label, package, treat, process, sell or advertise any food in a manner that is false, misleading or deceptive or is likely to create an erroneous impression regarding its character, value, quantity, composition, merit or safety."
- **SFCR s. 9(1):** "Any food that is sent or conveyed from one province to another or that is imported or exported and for which a standard is set out in the Standards of Identity Document must comply with that standard." **s. 9(2):** "Any food that is sent or conveyed from one province to another or that is imported or exported and that is likely to be mistaken for a food for which a standard is set out in the Standards of Identity Document must comply with that standard."
- **SFCR s. 201:** "A food, whether prepackaged or not, that is sent or conveyed from one province to another or that is imported or exported, and whose label bears a common name printed in boldface type, but not in italics, in the Standards of Identity Document must meet any standard that applies in respect of that common name."
- **SFCR s. 199(1)(b):** labelling is false or misleading if it uses "any expression, word, number, depiction or symbol that may reasonably be considered to imply that a prepackaged food contains any matter that it does not in fact contain or that it does not contain any matter that it does in fact contain."
- **FDR B.01.006.1:** "if a prepackaged product is likely to be mistaken for another food, words describing the true nature of the prepackaged product so as to distinguish it from the other food shall appear on the principal display panel".

### 5.2 CFIA guidance (tier a)
**CFIA dairy labelling page** (https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/dairy, modified 2026-05-28):
> "As with all foods with a standard of identity, only those foods that meet all the provisions set out in the standard can use the prescribed common name [201, SFCR; B.01.001(1)]. For dairy products that do not fall under a standard, the appropriate common name is the name by which it is generally known or a name that is not generic and that describes the food"
> "A dairy product that deviates from a prescribed standard may not use the common name associated with that standard unless the standardized common name is modified to indicate how the food differs in every respect from the food described by the standard."
> Example given by the CFIA: "If chives are added to the sour cream, the common name would need to be modified … for example, sour cream with chives."

**CFIA common name page** (https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/common-name, modified 2025-01-15), under "Modified standardized common names":
> "A modified common name of a standardized food may not be used to describe a food that does not meet that standard, unless the following conditions are met: it must always be clear to consumers that the food so described does not meet the standard; the consumer is told, in all respects, on the label and in advertisements, the way in which the food does not meet the standard. This information must always be in evidence in a clear and prominent manner as part of the common name on labels and in advertisements … The resulting food must resemble the standardized food in terms of characteristic taste, appearance and texture."
> "When a prescribed common name contains several words, additional descriptive words are not permitted to appear between the prescribed words which make up the common name."
> Imitations and substitutes: "An imitation food resembles the food imitated in flavour, texture, appearance and nutritional value. A substitute food does not have to physically resemble the food for which it substitutes … In some cases, the words 'imitation', 'substitute' or 'simulated' may be required to appear as part of the common name. For example, the common name of a simulated meat product must be the common name of the meat product that is simulated, modified by the word 'simulated', such as 'simulated chicken' [B.01.100(1), FDR]."

### 5.3 What we could and could not find
- **"Cottage cheese product":** no FDR, SFCR, CFCS, CSI or CFIA guidance text opened today defines or prescribes it. It is not a boldface common name. Legally it can only be a name under limb (c) of the "common name" definition ("a name that is not generic and that describes the food") or a modified standardized name. Whether any particular "cottage cheese product" label meets the CFIA's modified-name conditions is **a CFIA determination; we make no such finding**.
- **"Dairy product":** this is a defined *category* (SFCR s. 1), not a product name. We found no rule telling a non-standard curd product to be called "dairy product".
- **FDR imitation or substitute rules for cheese:** a search of the current FDR text found **no** "imitation", "substitute" or "simulated" rule for cheese or dairy. B.01.100 (simulated meat) is the example the CFIA uses.
- **Plant-based alternatives:** the CFIA's 2024 proposed guidance (consultation 29 Jul to 28 Oct 2024) covers **egg products only**: "This guidance does not address labelling or representation of other plant-based alternatives." https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/transparency/consultations-and-engagement/completed/label-and-represent-plant-based-alternatives/proposed-guidance (modified 2024-10-29).

The physical label is the only authority. **Check it in store before saying anything.** Do not infer compliance or non-compliance from the ingredient list against the (unverified) List 4 rows.
